<!--
Unfortunately, it’s not always straightforward to use TypeScript. In my experience there have been three main technical challenges teams generally face:
The onboarding flow: can you convert a project to TypeScript bit-by-bit, not with a huge upfront switch-over?
Type system power: can TypeScript’s type system understand the weird code you’ve been writing?
tsc speed: can TypeScript’s type checker analyze your project quick enough to not be annoying in CI and in your editor?
It’s 2025 now. Are these challenges solved today?
-->

<!--
TypeScript is a shockingly configurable language. If you want, your code can be what we would affectionately call “weakly” typed: where many values don’t have known types. That’s common for projects that haven’t had the time to be migrated towards the other end of the spectrum, “strongly typed”. 
TypeScript is technically a “gradually typed” language: it allows you to specify a strictness that matches where you want to be on that spectrum.
-->

<!--
Outside of Node.js, we have other runtimes, in particular Bun and Deno. They support running on TypeScript source files out-of-the-box. Which is really nice – no need to manage loaders, or install extra dependencies, or any of that.
Wouldn’t it be nice if Node.js had this too?
“Bun”: https://bun.sh
“Deno”: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deno_(software)#/media/File:Deno_Logo_2024.svg
-->

<!--
…but what happens if the AI messes up and gives an incorrectly typed response? Nothing verifies that it doesn’t lie to us. I’d feel very nervous about sending even a type union like this to an autocomplete-style AI, let alone a larger, more complex set of TypeScript types!
In other words, I want to attach TypeScript types to my AI prompts, and be guaranteed that the response will adhere to that type.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

</div>

<!--

-->

<!--
By the way, here’s something a little less useful. My SquiggleConf co-organizer Dimitri got Doom running in the TypeScript type system.
It’s hard to convey how ridiculous this accomplishment is. He built machine code garbage collection, memory compression, global value stack, dynamic dispatches, and generally a full WebAssembly runtime that took 12 days to render the first frame of Doom. What a maniac. Look at his happy face.
https://shop.squiggle.tools
https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0mCsluv5FXA&ab_channel=MichiganTypeScript
-->

<!--
Performance is such a bottleneck that we’ve seen multiple serious attempts to create TypeScript –or something like it- in a native speed language.
Just listing a few high-profile ones:
1. TypeRunner: an attempt in C++, halted in 2022 or so
2. Stc: this time in Rust, halted in 2023 or so
3. Ezno: another attempt in Rust, this time for a TypeScript-like language, but still early stage
Hot take: I think these are all doomed endeavors. No project I’ve seen in the wild has nearly enough funding to recreate all of TypeScript -let alone keep up with new TypeScript versions. If this is going to happen, it needs to come from TypeScript itself.
https://github.com/marcj/TypeRunner
https://stc.dudy.dev
https://kaleidawave.github.io/posts/introducing-ezno
-->

<!--
For example, one of my favorite rules that ships with ESLint is logical-assignment-operators. It’s one of many “syntax protip” rules that lets you know when there’s a better syntax for what you’re trying to do. In the case of this code:
friend = friend || ”me”;
…fun fact, there’s actually a new JavaScript “logical assignment operator” that does the same thing in fewer characters:
friend ||= ”me”;
The ESLint rule would look at the former, let you know there’s a better way, and -when prompted to- auto-fix to the latter.
A type checker would never tell you to make this switch. The code runs just as well after as it did before. The linter’s goal is not to enforce
-->

<!--
One of our type-checked rules, await-thenable, lets you know if you have an ‘await’ in front of something that doesn’t need to be awaited. Fun fact about JavaScript: ‘await’ directly gives back the value it’s provided if that value isn’t a Thenable (meaning, an object with a .then, such as a Promise).
await-thenable can let you know about an unnecessary await – and can suggest removing it for you.
This example is a little contrived (I’ve never seen someone await a console log) so let’s look at a bigger one.
-->

<!--
This code -written for a frontend framework you may not be familiar with- describes a button with a running state. The button receives an onClick prop. It sets up an internal running boolean for state. When clicked, it sets running to true, awaits the onClick, then sets running to false. Its DOM element is disabled while running.
The ‘await onClick()’ is unnecessary because onClick is typed as a function that returns void (meaning: nothing, ignore the result).
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---
